- N to C to G to J to I to Q also forms a path.
- N to B to G to J to I to Q forms another path via C and G through B.

- Removing the edge (C, N) will stop paths through C.
- Removing the edge (B, N) will stop paths through B.
- Removing the edge (B, G) will stop paths through G.

   - R: The immediate neighboring nodes of R are O, Q, and P.

3. **Identify paths between J and R**:
   - J connects to H, B, and E.
   - From H, there are no direct paths to R.
   - From B, there are connections to T, S, F, and A.

   - B connects to T, which connects to S, G, and C.

   - From P, it only connects to Q, which connects to R, O, G, and I.
   - From Q, it connects to I, G, and N.

   - B connects to other paths for transit. Its equivalent to J.

   - This can be achieved by removing outgoing edges from B and J.

6. **Check the solution**:

--- Model Response ---
To determine the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect the two black nodes \(A\) and \(H\), we need to analyze the paths between these nodes.

1. **Examine the two black nodes:** The black nodes are \(A\) and \(H\).

2. **Examine the immediate neighboring nodes of \(A\):** The immediate neighbors of \(A\) are \(E\), \(S\), and \(B\).

3. **Examine the immediate neighboring nodes of \(H\):** The immediate neighbors of \(H\) are \(G\), \(I\), and \(J\).

4. **Paths between \(A\) and \(H\):**
   - From \(A\), we can go to \(E\), \(S\), or \(B\).
   - \(E\) does not directly connect to any nodes that lead to \(H\).
   - \(S\) connects directly to \(B\).
   - \(B\) connects to multiple nodes: \(F\), \(G\), \(T\), and \(S\).

5. **Traversing from \(B\):**
   - \(B\) connects to \(G\), which in turn connects to \(J\), which connects to \(H\). This forms a path: \(A\)-\(B\)-\(G\)-\(J\)-\(H\).
   - \(B\) also connects to \(F\), which does not lead to \(H\).
   - \(B\) connects to \(T\), which does not lead to \(H\).

6. **Paths from \(H\):**
   - \(H\) connects to \(I\), which connects to \(Q\). There is no further path from \(Q\) to \(A\).
   - \(H\) connects to \(J\), which connects to \(G\), \(N\), \(C\), and \(O\). \(G\) is already evaluated to connect to \(A\) via \(B\). \(N\), \(C\), and \(O\) do not lead to \(A\).

7. **Evaluating the connections:**
   - We have \(A\)-\(B\)-\(G\)-\(J\)-\(H\) as a connecting path.
   - For disconnecting \(A\) and \(H\), we need to remove all possible paths connecting them.

8. **Identifying the bottleneck:**
   - The removal of edge \((A, B)\) would reduce paths to \(A\) to only \(E\) and \(S\), which do not lead to \(H\).
   - The removal of edges \((B, G)\) and \((H, J)\) would further ensure that \(G\) and \(J\) cannot connect \(A\) and \(H\).

9. **Check for the minimum cut set:**
   - Removing \((A, B)\) disconnects \(A\) from \(B\), thus terminating direct access from \(A\) to \(H\).
   - Further, since \(B\) acts as a hub, removing \((B, G)\) ensures that \(H\) through \(J\) cannot access \(A\).

Therefore, we conclude that the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(A, B), (B, G)].

   - From R to Q, Q connects to I, J, and P.
   - From J to I and G.

   - The paths from H to R can be traced via G and B or via I and Q.
   - The connections via B and G or via I and Q indicate potential bottlenecks.

5. **Evaluate the bottlenecks**:
   - Removing B will disconnect H from the left part of the graph, but it will still connect via G.
   - Removing G will disconnect H from the right part of the graph, but it will still connect via B.
   - Removing I will disconnect H from R via Q.
   - Removing Q will disconnect R from H via I.

To completely disconnect H and R, we need to remove nodes that ensure no path exists between them. The minimal set of nodes to achieve this is removing both G and I, as this will cut off all possible paths between H and R.
